===PAGE 2=== (ocr, 26 lines)
   1|                                                               In Memoriam
   2|                                                                        Vera A. (Ingemic l Mo Carthy
   3|                                                                      Vera resided in Lunenburg for 45 years with her husband. She
   4|                                                                      owned and operated Associated Data Concepts in litchburg. She
   5|                                                                      had a love for antiques and her home.                                        Her family and
   6|                                                                     grandchildren were the most important things in her life.                                          She
   7|                                                                      wrote a column "Observation Point" for the Lunenburg Ledger
   8|                                                                      Newspaper and was a member of the Lunenburg Historical
   9|                                                                      Commission. Before joining her husband in running their
  10|                                                                      company, she worked for many years as a secretary at the Thomas
  11|                                                                      Passios School.
  12|                                                                        Caroline C. (Fischer) Porter
  13|                                                                      Caroline, wife of Paul, was a longtime resident of Lunenburg and
  14|                                                                      co-owner of Hearthstone Realty. For many years, from 1980 to
  15|                                                                      1998 she was a member of the Cemetery Commission. She
  16|                                                                      enjoyed crocheting blankets for homeless veterans and scarves for
  17|                                                                      Crocker Elementary School in Fitchburg: She loved to summer in
  18|                                                                      Maine, but most of all she loved spending time with her family.
  19|                                                                           Wellium George MaaMunn Fa
  20|                                                                         Bill was an entrepreneur and patent-holder. After his retirement
  21|                                                                          he worked as the Cemetery Superintendent in Lunenburg,
  22|                                                                         courier driver and school bus driver. Bill was instrumental in
  23|                                                                          the establishment of the Mossman Teen Center and was on the
  24|                                                                         ground floor of the weekly newspaper, the Lunenburg Ledger.
  25|                                                                          He enjoyed hunting and fishing and watching his grandchildren
  26|                                                                         participate in sports, concerts and plays. Most of all, he enjoyed time with his sons.

   1|                                                                                 BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   2|                        Following the Town Election in May 2011, board reorganized with Dave Matthews elected as
   3|                        Chairman, Tom Alonzo as Vice Chair and Ernie Sund as Clerk. Paula Bertram and Steve
   4|                        deBettencourt being the other two members.
   5|                        In 2011 the board continued forward progress on existing and/or began efforts on the following
   6|                        projects:
   7|                             • Returning the Jones House to the tax rolls with a second RFP process with the continued
   8|                                    guidance of the Historical Commission
   9|                              • Moving the DPW renovation project forward with revised funding in face of increased
  10|                                    material and labor costs
  11|                             • Fully enacted the Municipal Energy Aggregation Plan with Colonial Power Group with
  12|                                    participating citizens enjoying reduced electric supply costs
  13|                             • Continued support for the redevelopment of the old Primary School into affordable senior
  14|                                    housing with the developer working to obtain Federal Housing and Urban Development
  15|                                    funding
  16|                              • Moved forward with the cost effective renovation and reorganization of Town Hall to
  17|                                    improve accessibility and extend its useful life using funding from the sale of the surplus
  18|                                    Susan Brown House.
  19|                             • Implemented use of the DPW Director's Pavement Management Plan ensuring limited
  20|                                    paving dollars are spent as effectively and fairly as possible
  21|                              • Moved the Summer Street redesign and redevelopment forward with engineering firm
  22|                                    VHB in conjunction with Leominster and Fitchburg so a construction ready project will
  23|                                    qualify for state grant funding to improve this area aesthetically and with an eye to future
  24|                                    development.
  25|                             • After a full RFP process, entered into a long term contract with Casella Waste to provide
  26|                                   garbage and recycling service to town participants saving $60,000 per year in cost
  27|                             • Drained the tanks at the abandoned service station at 925 Mass Ave to avoid environmental
  28|                                   damage with the costs liened to the property.
  29|                             • Signed a state wide mutual aid agreements for Public Safety and DPW allowing for shared
  30|                                   resources as needed and as made available
  31|                            • After much research and study agreed to regionalize Public Safety Dispatch avoiding new
  32|                                    extensive costs in state mandated staffing requirements while enabling a robust operation.
  33|                              • Dealt with another crippling storm in October which caused extensive damage to power
  34|                                   system and trees requiring a significant, expensive clean up effort. Lessons learned in the
  35|                                   2008 storm were enacted across Public Safety and DPW lightening the load significantly.
  36|                        2012 will be another challenging year with finances at the core or every decision and effort. As the
  37|                        town continues to work with revenue growth out of pace with national inflation, additional cuts
  38|                        and reductions will be unavoidable with the public feeling the brunt of lost services and response.

===PAGE 11=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                                                                           REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                          Progress was made on many levels within the Town in 2011, from approving a Pavement Management
   3|                          Plan, to entering into a long-term contract for solid waste, to building renovations, and joining a regional
   4|                          emergency dispatch district. In short, many advances were made to ensure that the Town is addressing
   5|                          critical needs.
   6|                          We continue to work on addressing long overdue building issues. By the end of 2011, we had completed
   7|                         interior renovations at the Town Hall, to provide better, more efficient working space for our
   8|                         administrative offices. These renovations also included providing a more secure environment for our
   9|                         technology room and providing ADA compliant restrooms. By the end of 2011, we were also substantially
  10|                         complete with major renovations and expansion at our DPW Facility on Chase Road. Included in the
  11|                         project was a full renovation of the existing building, often referred to as "the Barn." The existing garage
  12|                                         retrofitted with a modern and compliant HVAC system and electrical and lighting systems,
  13|                         which provide for a greatly improved working environment. New office space was also created within the
  14|                         existing space, also provides conference meeting space for the Boards & Committees associated with the
  15|                         Department of Public Works. If you haven't yet had a chance to tour the facility, please stop by and see it.
  16|                         The facility is spectacular. The DPW Building Committee did a fantastic job on a very small budget.
  17|                         We continue to work on a plan to completely rehabilitate the Summer Street corridor. In 2011, the Town
  18|                         approved funding for an Alternatives Analysis to determine which roadway realignment would work best
  19|                         for both residential properties and the enhancement of commercial properties.                                                 The Board elected to
  20|                         pursue a realignment which provides for one lane of travel in each direction, with center turn lanes at
  21|                         certain intersections to enhance vehicular safety. The next step in moving the project forward is to secure
  22|                         funding for design. At the 2012 Annual Town Meeting, the Town will be asked to appropriate funding for
  23|                         Lunenburg's share of the 25% design fee. Achievement of this milestone will allow the project's
  24|                         construction to be programmed in the area Transportation Improvement Plan (TIP). Construction of the
  25|                         project will be fully grant funded through the TIP.
  26|                         This year, we also launched a new Town website, with the assistance of both staff and a citizens task force.
  27|                         The new website is more user friendly, both for the public and staff, and certainly more eye appealing.
  28|                         The Town will continue to make improvements to the website, by adding additional content and
  29|                        functionality.
  30|                         During 2011, the Town entered into a 10-year contract for solid waste services with Casella Waste
  31|                         Systems, Inc. of Auburn, MA. This contract was structured in a way that citizens should see almost no
  32|                        increase in the cost of trash bags through its life. We were also able to modify the pickup schedule to a 5-
  33|                        day per week schedule, which means all trash pickup will occur earlier in the day and the trucks will be
  34|                        off the road during peak commuting times.
  35|                         Finally, the single greatest achievement in 2011 was the formation of the Nashoba Regional Emergency
  36|                        Dispatch District, of which Lunenburg is a founding partner. Creation of this district has been years in
  37|                         coming and will provide the Town, along with its other founding partners- Devens, Harvard and
  38|                         Lancaster- superior, efficient and cost-effective Emergency 911 Dispatch, in a start of the art facility.
  39|                         Creation of the district pools together the resources of all the partnering communities as well as
  40|                        substantial grant funding from the State. It is estimated that each founding member will save
  41|                         approximately $100,000 in annual operating costs, while at the same time meeting a new mandate which
  42|                         requires Emergency Medical Dispatch. If Lunenburg had to meet this mandate on its own, the increase in
  43|                        local operations would have been in excess of $100,000. This effort would not have been possible without
  44|                        the support of the Fire Chief & Department, the Police Chief & Department, the Dispatcher's Union,
  45|                         Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee.
  46|                         I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their
  47|                         time and many talents to make Lunenburg a better place. Thank you all for your dedicated service.
  48|                                                                                                 8

  35|                                                                                           CAPITAL PLANNING
  36|                        Lunenburg's Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year (FY) Plan and a Capital
  37|                        Improvement Plan. These Plans are to be presented to the Town Manager.
  38|                        As noted in the past reports the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis, Cost Effectiveness and
  39|                        Ability to Fund. The Committee has to review each request under these six elements. Not only do they have to determine that
  40|                        the request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize the level that meets the criteria. Therefore, not all requests are
  41|                        included in the FY Plans, mostly due to funding. The result of the proposed FY Plans provides the needed service for safety and
  42|                        protection of the public, the upkeep to our schools and the management of government.
  43|                        At the Annual Town Meeting May 2011, $538,765.00 was approved for Capital Requests. $133,765.00 was transferred from
  44|                        available funds and $405,000.00 was borrowed.
  45|                                                                                                10

  23|                                                                    PLANNING BOARD OPEN SPACE SUB-COMMITTEE
  24|                        Fiscal Year 2011 marked a very important milestone for the Open Space Sub-Committee. The Commonwealth's Executive Office
  25|                        of Energy and Environmental Affairs Division of Conservation Services approved the revision to the Town's Open Space and
  26|                        Recreation Plan (OSRP). The OSRP gathered input and feedback from Town boards, organizations, and residents about the
  27|                        open space, conservation, and recreation needs. The Plan establishes an inventory of the resources our community already
  28|                        has, and creates an action plan for making improvements. This Plan was first drafted in 1978, and was subsequently revised and
  29|                        updated in 1985, 1990, and 1998.
  30|                        Besides being an important step in addressing our Town's open space and recreation needs, having a state-approved Open
  31|                        Space and Recreation Plan makes the Town eligible to apply for certain State grants; these grants can reimburse the Town for
  32|                        up to 62% of the cost of open space and recreation acquisitions and improvements. Historically, the Town made regular use of
  33|                        these State grants, and much of Town-owned open space in Town was purchased and protected using these funds. However,
  34|                        the Town has not taken advantage of these grants since 1999. The State approval of the newly-revised Plan is valid for seven
  35|                        years.
  36|                        Looking forward, the Open Space Sub-Committee transitioned from a group charged with developing a plan into the Town's first-
  37|                        ever advisory committee responsible for guiding the implementation of the Plan. As such, the Open Space Sub-Committee also
  38|                        began consideration of the first action items outlined in the OSRP. The Plan identifies numerous improvements to our existing
  39|                        open space and recreation infrastructure, policies, and bylaws, and the action items were designed intentionally to be cost-
  40|                        neutral to the Town. The sub-committee will be working with the community and other Town boards and committees in the
  41|                        coming year to begin implementation.
  42|                        Marion M. Benson, Planning Director/Chair                                        Robert Verge, Conservation Commission
  43|                        Richard A. Brockelman, Member At Large                                           Sharon L. Jordan, Conservation Commission
  44|                        Brandon L. Kibbe, Member At Large                                               Janice P. Carrier, Conservation Commission

  14|                                                                            CENTRAL MASS MOSQUITO CONTROL
  15|                        The Central Massachusetts Mosquito Control Project (CMCP) currently provides its services to 39 cities and towns throughout Middlesex and Worcester Counties. The Project's headquarters is located at 111 Otis St., Northborough, MA. Project personnel are
  16|                        available to meet with any town board or resident to discuss the Project's procedures and activities. Our phone number is (508) 393-
  17|                                    CMMCP practices Integrated Mosquito Management (IMM), blending state of the art methods and techniques with
  18|                        effective mosquito control. IMM encourages the use of non-chemical means to accomplish the goal of mosquito reduction. expertise, experience, and scientific research to provide our member communities with modern, environmentally sound, cost
  19|                                    The Mosquito Awareness program which we offer to elementary schools in our district is very popular. Project staff meets
  20|                        with students and teachers to discuss mosquito biology, mosquito habitat, and control procedures. Much of the presentation is directed towards what the children and their families can do to prevent mosquitoes from. breeding around their homes. Slides,
  21|                        videos, handouts, and coloring books help to make this an interesting program. As part of our effort to reduce the need for pesticides, our first line of control is our ditch maintenance program. By
  22|                        cleaning clogged and         overgrown waterways, mosquito breeding can be reduced, drainage areas are returned to historical
  23|                        conditions, and water quality is improved. Source reduction, the elimination or reduction of a mosquito breeding source (i.e. water- holding containers), is practiced by alerting residents and business owners about potential mosquito producing sites they have
  24|                        created. They are informed about basic mosquito biology, and the need to eliminate these man-made breeding sources. A new
  25|                        program for CMCP is a source reduction program, i.e. tire recycling. Used tire casings are a preferred habitat for several species of human-biting mosquitoes, species that also carry disease. CMCP has implemented a program to be offered in several variants to
  26|                        remove these habitats from the environment.
  27|                        reduction or wetland restoration is not feasible. We have an extensive database of known breeding sites, and we encourage the Bacterial larval control with Bti (Bacillus thuringiensis) is used to treat areas where mosquito larvae are found and source
  28|                        only if pre-determined thresholds of mosquito larvae are exceeded. Bti is a naturally occurring product, and is exclusive to mosquito public to notify us of any areas they suspect could breed mosquitoes. Our field crews will investigate all requests and treat the area
  29|                                    Our goal is to handle all mosquito problems with wetlands restoration, source reduction or larval control, but we recognize
  30|                        that there are times when adult mosquito spraying is the only viable solution. In such cases residential areas are treated with either hand-held or pick-up truck mounted sprayers. Applications are site-specific and are determined by weather, type of area and
  31|                         mosquito population levels. These applications are initiated only by request of town residents. A phone notification system has been
  32|                        installed to announce potential spray areas for member cities and towns on their scheduled evening, and this information is listed on our website.
  33|                                    The project's surveillance program monitors adult mosquito and larval population density, and is the backbone for
  34|                         prescribing various control techniques. We have expanded the adult collection program to monitor for West Nile Virus in our service
  35|                         area. Specialized traps are used as a mobile force for viral monitoring, and are placed in member towns on a weekly basis for routine
  36|                         sampling. If a WNV or EEE hot spot is identified, surveillance is intensified to sample mosquitoes in that area and these collections are sent in to the Mass. Dept. of Public Health for virus testing.
  37|                                                                                                 13

  27|                                                                                    HISTORICAL COMMISSION
  28|                       The Lunenburg Historical Commission was created to identify, safeguard and help preserve for posterity, the unique physical
  29|                       assets of the Town as exemplified by the various sites, buildings and other edifices of historic, architectural or archeological
  30|                       significance to the Town of Lunenburg
  31|                                   The Mission Statement above continues to be the guiding principle for the Historical Commission to focus its efforts
  32|                       and work on a number of projects of historical consequence for the Town. The main initiatives implemented this year are as
  33|                       follows:
  34|                       Jones House - A Request for Proposal was published by the Town in anticipation of the sale of the Jones House at 42 Main
  35|                       Street as a historic property. No substantive bids were received and the Selectmen chose to re-advertise the property with some
  36|                       minor revisions to the Proposal form. Those bids were expected in early 2012 and the expectation will be to proceed with the
  37|                       sale of the Jones House as a first step in preserving this historic property and returning it to the tax rolls of the Town.
  38|                       Lunenburg Ledger - The program of periodic newspaper articles has continued to inform Town residents of the unique historical
  39|                       qualities of life in Lunenburg.
  40|                       Demolition Delay Requests - The Commission continued to work closely with the Building Inspector to review and determine the
  41|                       appropriate course of action for properties seeking demolition permits. During the year 4 properties were reviewed and acted
  42|                       Computerized Database - The development of the town wide computerized database utilizing the Massachusetts Historical
  43|                       Commission website continued. Enhancing the database with photos of the historic properties has been enabled through the
  44|                       use of the Board of Assessors online photo inventory which saves both time and expense. The completion of the web based
  45|                       resource will be the main focus of the upcoming year's activity.
  46|                       New Members - Two new members were welcomed to the Commission. Damon McQuaid replaced Vera McCarthy and Amory
  47|                       Phelps replaced Chriztine Foltz.
  48|                                                                                                17

   9|                                                                                         FIRE DEPARTMENT
  10|                       The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the highest
  11|                       quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
  12|                       As I begin to write my final annual report as your Fire Chief, I want to remind everyone of the importance of smoke and carbon
  13|                       monoxide detectors. These detectors are required by law but more importantly, can save your life. These detectors will alarm
  14|                       very early in a smoke or carbon monoxide event and will provide you time to escape from the building. Please never go to bed
  15|                       knowing your detectors are not working. YOU MAY NOT WAKE UP! Should you need help to test or install your detectors, call
  16|                       us- we will be glad to help you.
  17|                       This year we saw Tri Town Landing and Emerald Place develop into multi-unit housing. Tri Town currently has two fully
  18|                       occupied 32-unit buildings, Emerald Place has five 24-unit buildings, five 2-unit and two 4-unit buildings. Both developments are
  19|                       fully occupied as of this report. Emerald Place continues to build two and four-unit buildings and Tri Town is projecting
  20|                       construction of one more 32-unit building. Although progressing less rapidly, we still see other building projects in the planning
  21|                       stages in town. These projects combined with our other inspections and plan reviews keep the fire prevention office very busy.
  22|                       Town meeting provided us the funding to replace our brush truck's body (Engine 2). The1988 truck itself had very low mileage
  23|                       and was excellent condition. The body on the truck needed replacement to meet our current needs. The truck was sent out at the
  24|                       end of January and should be back in service the first week in March. This work will make the truck much more functional as a
  25|                       forestry unit. The new body will also be able to be removed and reused when the chassis of the truck needs to be replaced. Town
  26|                        meeting also provided funding for upgrading the infrastructure of the police departments radio system and replacement of some
  27|                       portable radios.
  28|                        Once again this year our department saw an increase in calls for service. As our town grows we will continue to see these
  29|                       requests increase. Our department remains committed to a combination of full and part time call firefighters and EMTs. We are
  30|                        obligated to address weekend and overnight coverage in the station for the safety of our community. Currently we staff the
  31|                        station from 6 am to 6 pm weekdays then depend on our call and career staff to respond to emergency calls after hours and on
  32|                        weekends. Although we have extremely committed and dedicated firefighters and EMT's on call, it still takes time to respond to
  33|                       the station, pick up the equipment and then respond to your call for assistance. When we have firefighters in the station, we can
  34|                       put the first piece of equipment in service in a minute or less. When we respond from our homes, it takes an additional 3 to 6
  35|                       minutes to respond to your call for help. We can accomplish this with the hiring of more full time help or by using our call
  36|                       personnel on a per diem basis. In either case, we need to fund over night and weekend coverage for consistent response times
  37|                       24 x 7. If we were to staff the station over night and on weekends we would consider staffing with two firefighter/EMTs in order to
  38|                       enable the first medical or fire piece of equipment to respond immediately to your call, with the remainder of the call firefighters
  39|                       responding from home as the situation warrants.
  40|                                   This year our incidents totaled 1780 and are as follows:
  41|                                   Fires                    495                    Emergency Medical           808
  42|                                   Inspections              417                    Other                         60
  43|                       Our department continues to train for a wide variety of emergencies so we are as prepared as possible for the situations we are
  44|                       faced with. Keeping our training current is the only way we can be prepared. Even in these difficult economic times, funding
  45|                       training is not an option. The less we encounter certain emergencies, the more we need to train for them, not only for your safety
  46|                        and protection but for the firefighters' safety and survival. For example, we participated in a class regarding the new Hybrid
  47|                       technology in automobiles, in order to safely manage the hazards and extrication of victims of accidents involving hybrid
  48|                        automobiles.
  49|                                                                                                18

   1|                       We graduated two new recruits from the Mass Firefighting Academy call volunteer program again this year bringing our total

   3|                      work with an intern program through the Lunenburg High School as a recruiting tool to encourage the youth of our community
   4|                      to consider a career in emergency services.

  10|                      have served as Chief. The Lunenburg Fire Department is an incredible team. We have been through a lot together and we have
  11|                      accomplished even more. It has been a privilege and an honor to lead this department. I cannot say enough about the dedication

   8|                          It is with great pride I submit my final Town Report to the residents of Lunenburg. After thirty-four years of Law Enforcement
   9|                          service of which over thirty years were served here in Lunenburg and twelve as your chief, I have decided to retire. My decision
  10|                          to leave was reached with a heavy heart and not without considerable deliberation with my family. This decision is based heavily
  11|                          on family commitment, future employment hopes and life-long dreams and aspirations. I am extremely humbled to have served
  12|                          as a police officer, sergeant and chief of police for this community. I believe I've lead the department into the twenty-first
  13|                          effective law enforcement even during the most economically challenging times. century by example and have built into place the framework necessary to carry out our daily mission of community policing and
  14|                         It seems like just yesterday Chief Hector Morin handed over the keys to the office, then located at the old station on School Street and wished me well. The budget was in place and the department had experienced officers and office staff anxious to
  15|                          offer their support. It is those officers and office staff I would first like to thank. I've been fortunate over the years to be honored
  16|                          by the presence and support of so many dedicated police officers who in a moment's notice and without hesitation are willing to
  17|                          put their lives on the line for the call of duty. My personal thanks to these dedicated men and women.
  18|                          My thanks also go out to the personnel in the fire department, emergency medical staff and dispatchers whom I've worked with
  19|                         for the past thirty years. Your dedication to service goes beyond the call of duty and your friendship is so appreciated. I would
  20|                         also like to thank our town manager, board of selectmen, staff at the town hall and all the various boards, committees,
  21|                         commissions, school officials, DPW staff and department heads I've worked with over the years. Lunenburg is extremely
  22|                         fortunate to have so many public service professionals willing to work together for the good of the community. I would also like
  23|                         to thank the many residents and business owners who have offered their unconditional support and encouragement over the
  24|                         years. I cherish all the personal and professional relationships I've made over the years and I am thankful for the wisdom I've gained as a result of these relationships.
  25|                         This year also saw the retirement of Sergeant Ernest Gould. After 26 years of dedicated police service Ernie decided to head
  26|                         south to enjoy the good weather year round. Our thanks and best wishes to Sergeant Gould and his family for their commitment
  27|                        I am happy to report that Lunenburg remains a safe community in which to reside and visit. Violent crime remains at a minimum.
  28|                        I must credit our staff for their dedication and perseverance as our community continues to grow and calls for service and
  29|                        administrative duties increase. The staff here at LPD commits themselves each day to providing the best professional,
  30|                        responsive public service possible. We continue to work with town officials as part of a concerted effort to address our
  31|                        department's staffing and capital needs. We must strive to keep pace with the ever changing world of technology, the rapid
  32|                        town and regional population/commercial growth and the resulting increased demands for services made on our department.
  33|                        We continue to participate in regional domestic violence roundtables and high risk DV teams as well as the North Worcester
  34|                        County Drug Task Force in order to identify and deter domestic violence, drug activity and related crimes in our area. We also
  35|                        continue to hold regular roundtable discussions with our school administration, District Attorney's Office, Juvenile Court
  36|                        officials and social service departments in order to identify and address crime trends and issues challenging our student population.
  37|                        The department answered 19,750 calls for service and made 294 arrests in 2011. A portion of these calls for service include: 247
  38|                        abandoned/hang-up 911 calls, 157 animal complaints, 568 assist to the Fire/EMS Department, 1069 burglar alarms, 53 assaults,
  39|                        682 assists to citizens, 44 car breaks, 136 burglaries, 749 community policing events, 142 disturbances, 126 incidents of
  40|                        domestic abuse, 60 cases of harassment, 292 larcenies, 599 car accidents, 64 noise complaints, 34 neighbor disputes, 46
  41|                       annoying phone call complaints, served 116 restraining orders, 187 shopliftings, 894 cases of suspicious activity, 120 cases of
  42|                       vandalism and 329 well-being/health and welfare checks. Traffic related issues, motor vehicle accidents, shopliftings and
  43|                       larceny, domestic violence calls for service and drug related issues remain a continuing challenge for our staff.

   6|                                                                              DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   7|                      "The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the Town's infrastructure and related
   8|                      assets in a manner which meets and enhances the current and future social and economic needs of the community, to
   9|                      contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for the Town's citizens and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and
  10|                      economically sound means of disposing of solid waste and wastewater"
  11|                      Public works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance
  12|                      services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel and equipment to best serve the residents of the community
  13|                      through the effective delivery of many services.
  14|                      PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  15|                      Consistent with the prioritization as determined by our Pavement Management Plan, road improvements were initiated on New
  16|                      West Townsend Rd., Lancaster Ave., and a limited section of Northfield Rd. to the Fitchburg line. The scope of the project
  17|                      included: a leveling course of asphalt followed by a top or finish course, sections of "berm" or curbing, shoulder work, drainage
  18|                      improvements and driveway aprons. As an issue of public safety, a leveling course was installed on Pleasant view Ave.
  19|                       Expanding the scope of our pavement preservation methods we added "crack sealing" to the overall program. This process
  20|                      involves the installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure to cracks or separations in the pavement. This application
  21|                       can add approximately 5 years or more to the existing surface. The aforementioned Pavement Management Plan also identifies
  22|                      specific areas where this method is the best option.
  23|                       Funding continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our roadways. The primary source of funding
  24|                      is the Massachusetts Department of Transportation Chapter 90 program. The estimated value of our 90 miles of paved streets is,
  25|                      by industry standards, is calculated at 62 million dollars. The General Highway maintenance line within the operational budget
  26|                      remains stagnant. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement program a substantial infusion of funds is necessary. A
  27|                      conservative estimate would be approximately 1.2 million dollars per year total or an additional $800,000.00 to existing
  28|                      allocations.
  29|                      Without additional funding our roads Town-wide will continue to deteriorate to a point where potentially the safety of our
  30|                      travelling public could be compromised.
  31|                      Line Markings
  32|                      For the first time in many years, adequate funding was made available to provide center line striping and fog lines at the curb on
  33|                      all our major roadways. Further, where applicable, crosswalks, stop bars and designated parking areas were repainted.
  34|                      FLEET MAINTENANCE
  35|                      The DPW is responsible for the fueling and repair of all Town vehicles and equipment excluding the Fire Dept. apparatus. High
  36|                      performance police units, park, cemetery, and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are
  37|                      serviced by department personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent. Due to the volatile global
  38|                      oil market, the overall cost of fuel and all petroleum based products continues to be a moving target.
  39|                      FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  40|                      The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  41|                                       DPW Garage 520 Chase Road                          Public Safety Complex
  42|                                       Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                          Town Hall
  43|                                       Cemetery Bldg. Holman St.                          Eagle House

  46|                                       Old Primary School                                 Jones House
  47|                                      Veterans Park                                      All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
  48|                      Due to limited resources, only one individual is dedicated to the oversight of ALL municipal buildings. Much of the preventative
  49|                      maintenance is outsourced while daily work orders are handled by the facilities manager who remains on call 24/7 with minimal
  50|                      support.
  51|                      ENVIRONMENTAL
  52|                      Storm Water
  53|                                                                                             21

===PAGE 25=== (ocr, 54 lines)
   1|                           The Environmental Protection Agency has not yet set the requirements for Phase I! Storm water regulation and permits. As we
   2|                           await the potential of yet another unfunded Federal mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol. It is difficult
   3|                           to become proactive with such limited resources.
   4|                           The DPW is forced to become reactionary to roadway drainage issues. A capital program in the 1990's earmarked $20,000.00
   5|                           annually toward rehabilitation/installation of piping and catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding placed the
   6|                           financial burden upon the overall General Highway maintenance allocation effectively reducing drainage repairs and roadway
   7|                           repairs as well.
   8|                           As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed. Little or no preventative
   9|                           maintenance, short of catch basin cleaning can be done.
  10|                           Waste Water
  11|                           The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system from permitting, inspections, and daily preventative maintenance
  12|                           of collector lines and pumping stations to billing and documentation. There is but one full time person who diligently remains on
  13|                           call, 24/7 365 for all sewer related emergencies. This is an enormous responsibility for one individual.
  14|                           The "capped" landfill off Young's Rd. is in compliance with the Division of Environmental Protection. Twice yearly the site is
  15|                           monitored for excessive methane gas as well as the possibility of groundwater contamination. The perimeter fencing is
  16|                          inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  17|                           Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be hazardous waste.
  18|                           We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP
  19|                          requires yearly testing of these materials.
  20|                          On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of brush,
  21|                          leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents.
  22|                          Solid Waste
  23|                           Public works orders, stores and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by
  24|                           our current vendor, Casella.
  25|                          In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned over ownership of the dam
  26|                          to the Town. After completion of the reconstruction process, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping
  27|                          was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association with yearly assistance from the Lunenburg
  28|                          Fire Dept. Approximately five years ago custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of
  29|                          an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was determined to come under the umbrella of the Department of
  30|                          Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance with current and future regulations is
  31|                          critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates.
  32|                          WINTER OPERATIONS
  33|                           Seasonal expenditures continue to exceed allocations resulting in deficit spending. During the winter months there is no task
  34|                          more important to public safety than clearing the roadways, sidewalks and municipal lots of ice and snow in an effort to assure
  35|                          the safe passage of pedestrian and vehicular traffic.
  36|                          As first responders, DPW personnel with the assistance of dedicated subcontractors, work tirelessly from start to finish
  37|                          regardless of the severity or duration of the event. Nowhere is our performance more critically evaluated. Plow drivers are
  38|                          required to remain vigilant and on call throughout the winter season with no additional compensation.
  39|                         In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners one DPW employee, a Park Superintendent, oversees and maintains
  40|                          all parks and grounds exclusive of school property. Support staff and equipment is provided by public works. The new multi-
  41|                          use track complex remains under the control of the Commissioners with maintenance support from DPW personnel.
  42|                          CEMETERY
  43|                          One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DW employee, handles the duties of the North and South cemeteries, maintaining the
  44|                         grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies
  45|                         equipment and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year.
  46|                         As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow Town employees as we
  47|                          persevere through these difficult times.
  48|                         "As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of Lunenburg for
  49|                         future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with the challenges of each of the
  50|                          divisions under the umbrella of public works"

  33|                                                                                      SEWER COMMISSION
  34|                      The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006, with the Board of
  35|                      Selectmen serving as Sewer Commissioners during the construction and start-up of the sewer system. The Sewer Commission
  36|                      bears the responsibility of overseeing the Town's sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and implementing rules,
  37|                     regulations and policies, and through the DPW, manages and maintains the operational side of the system. Under the Town's
  38|                     the town manager. Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of
  39|                      The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 70% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the
  40|                     City of Leominster, and the other 30% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg. Due to increases in the rates charged by the City
  41|                     of Leominster and the City of Fitchburg, the Commission again increased sewer rates by 20%, effective January 2012. The
  42|                     Commission continues to review our system for ways to reduce our expenses and improve operations to keep our costs down.
  43|                    In addition, we are working to avoid major cost increases in the future by adopting policies and maintenance procedures.
  44|                                                                                             24

===PAGE 28=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                         Two major issues that affect costs and operations of the sewer system are grease traps and infiltration and inflow (I/l). The
   2|                        grease problems in their discharge lines, they will have to install an external grease trap to further project the sewer system,
   3|                        avoiding costly repairs to our sewer system and the systems of Fitchburg and Leominster as well. This policy was
   4|                        communicated to all food service establishments in the sewer district after a public hearing. The school department is affected
   5|                        and has successfully worked with the Commission to implement a realistic schedule and process to handle their grease.
   6|                        Bootleggers was the first restaurant to fall under the renovation clause and is assuring the Commission that its operation will
   7|                        also comply. Controlling grease protects the system and prevents higher rate increases for all users.                                Both Fitchburg and
   8|                        Leominster have begun implementing similar regulations. We will continue to educate homeowners on how to protect the
   9|                       infrastructure by reducing their disposal of fats, oil and grease into the system and avoiding blockages.
  10|                        The second major issue, I/l, is usually a result of groundwater entering the system through leaks in pipes and manholes, but can
  11|                        also come from the illegal connection of roof drains or sump pumps. The Commission has contracted with Weston & Sampson
  12|                        Engineering to address I/l issues and implement a flow metering program. Three new meters were installed at Massachusetts
  13|                        Avenue., Graham Street and Summer Street. Three temporary meters were also installed at other strategic locations in Town. The
  14|                       purpose of the meters was to monitor flow during high water events and help us find trouble areas that need attention. This
  15|                       testing was done in March and April and was followed up with smoke and dye testing in targeted areas. We were able to identify
  16|                       several sewer manhole construction and maintenance issues contributing to excess water entering the system, which have been
  17|                       addressed. The school department discontinued an old pipe from the Brooks House to the high school, and redirected a sump
  18|                       pump at the high school, further helping the system. However, while worthwhile, these actions only addressed a small portion
  19|                       of the excess water. Illegal sump pumps and leaking pipes leading from houses to the street are believed to be the major
  20|                       remaining issues. As a result, the Commission is communicating with residents in areas where there are known old pipes, and
  21|                       we will begin an inspection process of all homes connected to the sewer to assure that there are no extra sources of water.
  22|                       Several homes in the Whalom area, originally connected to the older Leominster sewer system, have already been required to
  23|                       connect to the Lunenburg system, discontinuing the use of a manhole and clay pipes found contributing to the l/l. We currently
  24|                       pay the City of Fitchburg and the City of Leominster for sewerage that is sent to them based on water meter readings of
  25|                       properties within the sewer district. But both the City of Fitchburg and the City of Leominster have stated that they will
  26|                       eventually start to charge the Town based on actual flows received into their systems, which could potentially increase our
  27|                      in the system. operational costs substantially. It is for this reason that the Commission will continue to actively work to reduce excess water
  28|                       In November, we contracted for a comprehensive inspection and analysis of all pump stations. While most were in excellent
  29|                      condition, upgrades for efficiency were made and preventive maintenance needs reviewed in order to keep them running
  30|                      effectively for many more years. As part of the DPW building renovation, our sewer technician, Corey Dowd, was provided a
  31|                      work station and computer to monitor the pump stations and flow meters for up-to-the-minute reports. In addition, throughout the year, Corey was also able to complete the 20 training credit hours required to renew his certification as Wastewater Plant
  32|                      Operator, which was due to expire in December 2011. This certification is good for another 2 years.
  33|                      The Commission continues to review several proposed developments (Highfields, Lunenburg Village) and some developments
  34|                      presently under construction (Stone Farm, Emerald Place) to ensure that the properties meet all requirements of the Sewer
  35|                      Commission bylaws and regulations. Most large projects require an engineered peer review by the commission's engineering
  36|                     consultant which is paid for by the developer to avoid additional cost to the Town. The Commission requested and received
  37|                     approval to extend the sewer district to accommodate a bisected commercial lot at 357 Electric Avenue which could not support a Title V septic system at the Special Town Meeting in December.
  38|                     The Commission is performing a comprehensive review of all its governing bylaws, regulations, policies, procedures and fees to
  39|                     assure that they are consistent and fair, meet all state and federal laws and allow us to be a state of the art operation. We will be
  40|                     continually seeking ways ways to increase the number of sewer users to support our operation while protecting the
  41|                    without sewers currently installed. environment. Part of this will include outreach to residents in areas listed as "to be sewered" on our sewer district map, but
  42|                    The Commission continues to work on inter-municipal agreements with the City of Fitchburg and the City of Leominster to assure adequate capacity for the future and address cost and operational issues.
  43|                    The Commission would like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director and his staff, Corey Dowd, Sewer Technician, and Jim
  44|                    Breault, Facilities Manager, who acts as back up technician, for their dedication, expertise and hard work. They can be counted
  45|                    on to keep the system in good shape and running effectively. The Commission would also like to thank Barb Lefebre for her dedication and hard work as the Business Manager for the system.
  46|                                                                                            25

   5|                                                                                    ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   6|                         The Zoning Board of Appeals heard many petitions during calendar year 2011. There were several applicants seeking variances,
   7|                         special permits and dimensional variations. The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and forth Wednesdays of the
   8|                         month on an "as needed" basis.
   9|                         The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Donald F. Bowen, Chairman, Raymond Beal, Vice Chairman, James
  10|                         Besarkarski, Alfred Gravelle, Hans Wentrup and Associate members, David Blatt, Paul Doherty and Sheila Lumi.                                              Lisa
  11|                         Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  12|                        The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  13|                                    To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  14|                                    To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  15|                              • To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  16|                                    amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from the
  17|                                    Administrative     Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other
  18|                                    Administrative Official.
  19|                              • To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 40B. The
  20|                                    Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under Chapter 40B by
  21|                                    developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent local ordinances.
  22|                        The Office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue. If you need
  23|                        any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421 during regular business hours.
  24|                        Zoning Board applications are available in the office of the Town Clerk and online at www.Lunenburgonline.com.

   6|                      Volunteer Recognition Program                                                One Stroke Painting

   8|                       This year, The Luneburg Gray Belts is a mixed martial arts senior self-defense program developed by Doreen Noble and Officer
   9|                       Mile Luth of the Lunenburg Police Department were nominated for the Innovator of the Year Award with the Massachusetts
  10|                       Council on Aging. The director, Doreen Noble was also nominate Advocate of the Year for her cable television program that
  11| a                     reaches out locally to elders in our town and surrounding municipalities.
  12|                       On March 7", the Boston Post Cane was presented to Lunenburg's oldest living resident, Ruth Green age 101. Ruth was
  13|                       surrounded by family, friends, and dignitaries. The ceremony went viral and can be viewed on You Tube. You can view it by
  14|                       entering; "Lunenburg's Oldest resident receives Boston Post Cane.
  15| IS                    Our funding comes from different sources. In FY11 we received a total of $118,460 from the municipal budget for salaries and
  16| le                    expenses. The Monachusett Area Regional Transit Authority (MART) funds the salaries of our van drivers and ½ time for our
  17|                       dispatcher. We also receive funding from the Massachusetts Department of Elder Affairs. The Friends of the Eagle House, Inc. a
  18|                       non-profit 501c3 organization is the fundraising group for the senior center. Their contributions are invaluable and they are ever
  19|                       ready to help us with things that cannot be covered by the municipal budget. Anyone wishing to make a donation to aid us in
  20|                       Drive, and Lunenburg. A donation to the Eagle House is a nice way to honor the memory of a loved one.
  21| ed                    Staffing at the Eagle House consisted of a full-time director; one 33 hour a week administrative assistant, one 19 hour a week Outreach worker and two food service coordinators who worked a total of 15 hours a week. We also have 2 van drivers who
  22| S.                    worked a combined total of 30-35 hours a week. Our staffing levels are minimal for a facility of our size, our programing levels
  23|                       are extremely high and the amount of services we provide is astronomical, therefore, volunteers are the backbone of the center,
  24|                       without them many of the services we provide would not be in place.
  25|                       Volunteer jobs range from meals drivers, to Friendly visitors, to kitchen assistants, and program facilitators. In addition we have
  26|                       wonderful people who donate knitted items that are sent to schools, hospitals maternity wards and the veteran's homes. Many
  27| S,                    of the Town's elderly not only volunteer at the Eagle House but, also give of their time and talents to the Town and other
  28| to                    community organizations. This year Eagle House volunteers clocked 8,162 hours of service. We also had 13 people participate
  29|                       in the Senior Tax Work Off program and they provided over 1,500 hours of labor to various town departments and the Lunenburg
  30|                       Public School System.
  31|                       Without the generosity and overwhelming support of the community many of the programs we are able offer would not be
  32| A.                    possible.
  33| ill)                                                                          GREEN COMMUNITY TASK FORCE
  34|                       This has been a very busy year for the Green Community Task Force. We continue to focus our efforts on providing information
  35|                       to Town officials and residents with respect to Green Energy alternatives. The largest undertaking this year is the ongoing work
  36|                       to educate Town officials and residents on the impact and rewards of implementing the Green Community Act in Lunenburg.
  37|                       The Task Force met on eleven occasions throughout the year and dealt with multiple Green topics ranging from solar electric
  38|                       and hot water generation, wind turbine energy, recycling opportunities, delivering of information and resources as well as the Green Communities Act.
  39|                       This year the project to install solar panels on Turkey Hill Middle School is complete. The panels are working well and estimated
  40|                       yearly savings for the Town will be $10,000. Another major project the Task Force has been working on is the proposal of a
  41|                        1.5MW solar farm on the old Town landfill located on Youngs Road. The project has gone out for bid and the contractor we have
  42|                       selected is working with UNITIL on the details to implement the system. Hopefully we will be able to start negotiating the final
  43|                       numbers for electricity cost for the Town shortly. We have been looking into ways of implementing energy saving methods at
  44|                       the schools through Public-Private Partnership (PPP) contracts.
