===PAGE 33=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                                                                SUPERINTENDENT'S MESSAGE
   2|                                                                            Loxi Jo Calmes, Superintendent of Schools
   3|                       We have had another productive year teaching and learning in the Lunenburg Public Schools. The individual reports that follow
   4|                       from each of the buildings and school directors chronicle the outstanding performance of our students, their many events and
   5|                       achievements. I was pleased to recognize an outstanding member of the class of 2012, Derek Booth, as this year's
   6|                       Superintendent's Award recipient. We continue to take great pride in the work of our dedicated faculty and staff and the
   7|                       accomplishments of our wonderful students. Ours is a caring community of learners working hard each day, learning and
   8|                       growing in a safe, nurturing environment.
   9|                       This past August the Lunenburg School Regionalization Committee unanimously voted to withdraw from discussion with the
  10|                      North Middlesex Regional School District (NMRSD) School Committee about joining the district in a regional expansion. The
  11|                       Committee worked for 15 months with a sub-committee of the NMRSD School Committee, and in July 2011 at a joint meeting, a
  12|                       detailed analysis of operational costs and benefits of an expanded region projected a positive economic impact for Lunenburg,
  13|                       but not for the NMRSD, resulting in a recommendation by their sub-committee to not pursue expanding the region. Lunenburg's
  14|                      Committee and the NMRSD sub-committee worked well together and in good faith, though the process was challenging at times.
  15|                       Regionalization was one option to address the critical facility needs of Lunenburg High School. These needs were documented
  16|                      in a master plan study in 2007 as well as the recent accreditation report which placed Lunenburg High School on warning status.
  17|                      To quote the Commission's report,           "The Lunenburg High School site and physical plant do not adequately support all aspects of
  18|                       the educational program and the support services for student learning. As a result of the age of the facility, the poor repair of
  19|                       the building, the limited technological resources, and limited space available in the building, the physical plant does not meet
  20|                       the demands of teaching and learning, and the basic expectations of a school's physical plant. These concerns dramatically
  21|                       limit the school's ability to meet the educational needs of students." The good news is that in January 2012 the Massachusetts
  22|                       School Building Authority moved Lunenburg High School into the eligibility phase to begin a partnership with the MSBA to
  23|                      identify a solution for the Lunenburg High School Facility.
  24|                       Given that three of the four schools in the district have significant facility needs, and to address MSBA concerns for the number
  25|                       of students served in a school, the School Committee voted to reconfigure the Lunenburg Public Schools from four to three
  26|                       buildings with a combined 6-12 school. A School Building Committee has been formed. We are very excited to have this
  27|                       opportunity to address the urgent needs of Lunenburg High School.
  28|                       Our schools are very fortunate to have a committed School Committee who dedicates countless hours in service to our students
  29|                       and education. These committed individuals work closely with members of other town boards and committees to ensure that
  30|                       our schools remain strong and responsive to the students we serve. We are also fortunate to have many involved parents who
  31|                       contribute in a variety of ways as classroom volunteers, in booster clubs, as Musicaiders and PTO members. The PTO, under
  32|                       the leadership of Wendy Bertrand as President again this year, is quite remarkable in the level of support it provides the
  33|                       students in our schools.
  34|                       Students, with the support of their teachers, continue to engage in learning about topics of concern to them through community
  35|                       service learning. This year students in Mr. Truax's Algebra math class at Lunenburg High School researched and are working to
  36|                       construct and maintain an ice rink for community use. Josephine Bowen represented our schools and community at Governor
  37|                       Patrick's Project 351 - 2012 day of service. Every day students across the district serve as mentors and coaches, and they
  38|                       continue to collect for the town food bank giving back to the community that has supported them and their education. The
  39|                       difficult financial times linger for many Lunenburg families, but through the efforts of our students and generosity of the
  40|                       community-at-large, help is provided. We are very proud of the students of our schools!
  41|                       This past year our schools bid farewell to two retirees who devoted many years to public service-their total tenure in
  42|                       Lunenburg represents 26 years of service. Retiring this past year were Scott Curtis, LHS tutor (13 years), and Judith Schenck,
  43|                       extend our best wishes to them in their retirement. This past year we bid farewell to Mike Barney when he accepted another
  44|                       position. I want to thank Mr. Barney for his 13 years of service. I also want to extend my appreciation to Mr. Joseph P. Dillon for
  45|                       serving our students as interim principal at LHS for the past year. Lunenburg is most fortunate to have such committed and
  46|                       caring educators, faculty and administrators.
  47|                                                                                       SCHOOL FACILITIES
  48|                                                                                          John Londa, Director
  49|                                                                                                30

===PAGE 34=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                    Long-range planning for renovations or replacement of Lunenburg High School moved forward following the termination of the
   2|                    investigation into the possibility of regionalization with North Middlesex Regional School District. In January 2012, the
   3|                    Massachusetts School Building Authority voted to move the Lunenburg High School project into the eligibility period for a
   4|                    project. A school building committee was formed by the Board of Selectmen in January 2012 to monitor the development of the
   5|                    project. In May 2012 at the annual town meeting, the school building committee will request funding in order to hire an Owner's
   6|                    Project Manager to oversee the scoping of the project, fund a designer to conduct a feasibility study and schematic design and
   7|                    fund testing needed to fully develop the scope of the project.
   8|                    The condition of the T.C. Passios Elementary School's 25-year-old roof remains a pressing concern for the School District. The
   9|                    FY13 capital plan was submitted with a recommendation to re-seam the rubber membrane roof in order to buy additional time on
  10|                    the existing roof as the School Department's highest priority.
  11|                    The focus of the school maintenance department continues to be efforts to stretch the life on the High School and Passios
  12|                    Elementary School roofs, to make improvements to the School Department's compliance with the American Disabilities Act
  13|                    (ADA), to upgrade security and to make improvements to energy efficiency.
  14|                    At Lunenburg High School, the front lobby area was renovated to create a secure entry; modifications continue to develop the
  15|                    front entry into an accessible ingress. Students from Montachusett Regional Vocational High School are fabricating railings to
  16|                    be installed in the spring of 2012 to complete a ramp from the sidewalk in front of the school to the main entry.
  17|                    A 102 Kilowatt solar photovoltaic system was completed at the Turkey Hill Middle School through the Energy Efficiency
  18|                    Conservation Block Grant Program using a power purchasing agreement with Broadway Electric, Inc. In June 2011 the system
  19|                    located on the classroom wing began energy production and will generate approximately 114,000 KWH a year (roughly il3 of the
  20|                    school's electricity consumption).
  21|                    In February 2011 Unitil funded an energy audit of the Passios Elementary School. A lighting upgrade project was developed from
  22|                    the audit using $13,973 in energy savings from the FY11 School Department operating budget and $32,627 in rebate funds from
  23|                    Unitil. The project was completed in August 2011 with the conversion of classrooms to 25 W T8 lamps and occupancy sensors.
  24|                    Gym lighting was replaced with T5 high bay fixtures and exterior lights were converted to LED based lighting. The total project
  25|                    cost was $46,660 with an estimated payback in 2.8 years based upon an estimated $5,200 savings.
  26|                    The Town FY12 capital program included asbestos repairs at Lunenburg High School and Passios Elementary School, front
  27|                    entry modification at Lunenburg High School, re-paving of the Turkey Hill Middle School parking lot with the addition of
  28|                    sidewalks to the track and to the classroom wing exits, and conversion of the blackboards to whiteboards at the Middle School.
  29|                                                                           INSTRUCTIONAL SERVICES OFFICE
  30|                                                                                      Karen Martin, Director
  31|                     During this past year, the Instructional Services Office continued to focus on three areas critical to the District's mission: (a)
  32|                    curriculum and assessment development, (b) continuous improvement for ALL and (c) meeting the academic, social and
  33|                     emotional needs of our students. Following is a summary of activities and results in each of these areas.
  34|                    Curriculum and Assessment
  35|                     This school year the Math Curriculum Taskforce developed the K-8 Math Assessments/Understanding by Design units. The 9th-
  36|                    12th curriculum is being developed in curriculum map format by individual course and aligned to the Common Core. Throughout
  37|                    the school year, each building has been involved in developing and refining common assessments, Understanding by Design
  38|                     units and curriculum maps. These tools will ensure students have common learning experiences and that there is regular
  39|                     monitoring of student performance against a standard. The Lunenburg High School has integrated school-wide rubrics to
  40|                     measure student learning expectations. During the 2011-12 school year, an English Language Arts Taskforce will develop a
  41|                     curriculum aligned with the Massachusetts Curriculum Framework for English Language Arts and Literacy which incorporates
  42|                    the Common Core State Standards for English Language Arts and Literacy in History/Social Studies, Science, and Technical
  43|                     Subjects. The new framework includes literacies for science and social studies.
  44|                     Our district uses our MCAS (Massachusetts Comprehensive Assessment System), our AYP (Adequate Yearly Progress) and the
  45|                     new Growth Model data to monitor overall performance and to provide specific feedback for teachers, students and our program.
  46|                     Analysis of the test data is done at the district, building, teacher and student level. We can draw some conclusions that will help
  47|                     us move forward and improve instruction for all students. Key findings:
  48|                           * Lunenburg Primary School met AYP and is on target to meet NCLB goals.
  49|                                                                                             31

===PAGE 35=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                                  * Thomas C. Passios met AYP in the aggregate and for all subgroups in ELA and Math and was a commended school for
   2|                                       growth.
   3|                                  * Turkey Hill Middle School did not meet AYP in the aggregate and in subgroups for ELA and Math. Turkey Hill Middle
   4|                                        School in 7th and 8th grade doubled the number of students in the Advanced level in ELA.
   5|                                  ** Lunenburg High School met the NCLB goals.
   6|                                  * The district has been successful in moving students into the proficiency levels. Our challenge is to move beyond the
   7|                                       state comparison to comparisons with the proficient and advanced levels.
   8|                                 * Our challenge is to accelerate improved student performance in all subjects through differentiation and personalization
   9|                                 * Meeting AYP for all of our subgroups, particularly with our students with disabilities and low income, continues to be
  10|                                       an area of focus for the school district. Our challenge is to develop instructional strategies and models of interventions
  11|                                       for students at risk and students with disabilities that significantly improve student performance.
  12|                           CONTINUOUS IMPROVEMENT FOR ALL
  13|                           The No Child Left Behind legislation provides an opportunity to carefully examine performance results for all students. This data
  14|                           helps guide the school councils, administrative team and Results teams to plan for appropriate interventions, programming and
  15|                           professional development.
  16|                           School structures and collaboration focused on student learning ensure all students improve. The Lunenburg Public Schools
  17|                           has Results Teams, Understanding by Design training, Pyramid of Success/Response to Intervention, a comprehensive
  18|                           professional development program and new teacher induction program to support student learning.
  19|                           Results Teams meet one time per month during faculty meeting time. The teams use data to set goals, plan teaching strategies
  20|                           and assess the results. This process ensures a regular review of student performance and effective teaching strategies.
  21|                           Through ongoing analysis of student results, refinement of units and increased awareness of effective assessments and
  22|                           instructional practices, student performance will improve. The UBD units provide additional measures in non-MCAS subjects
  23|                           and provide additional curriculum materials for new teachers. Community Service Learning projects are being developed to
  24|                           provide opportunities for the transfer of understandings to an authentic problem and need. The district received a number of
  25|                           CSL grants and presented at the state-wide conference on CSL with a presentation on start-up ideas for districts. 2223
  26|                           Ongoing and sustained professional development continues in a range of areas. This year graduate level courses were offered
  27|                           in Building Inclusive Practices, Healthy Update, Google and Teaching Like A Champion. Our new teacher induction program
  28|                           provides ongoing support for new teachers to the district. New teachers participate in a one-day orientation, regular meetings
  29|                           during the school year, and professional development for new teachers. A mentor team supported our new teachers within each
  30|                          of the buildings.
  31|                          GEMS - Academic, Social and Emotional Learning
  32|                           This year is the sixth year of implementation of the district-wide GEMS curriculum. The vision of GEMS is ensuring all students
  33|                           grow to their potential, develop empathy towards others, value the importance of self-discipline and become contributing
  34|                           members of the community. This year, all schools are working to establish school-wide routines and structures that support
  35|                           academic, social and emotional learning for ALL children. Teachers have been involved in professional development and
  36|                           Responsive Classroom training. On professional development days, nurses, counselors, and special educators have studied the
  37|                          needs of students with disabilities and are developing a Response to Intervention protocol.
  38|                          SUMMARY
  39|                          The Instructional Services Office has a strong partnership with the individual schools, the administrative team, faculty and
  40|                          community. The combined and integrated efforts of all parties ensure continued focus on improving student performance and
  41|                          strive to provide teachers, administrators, students and the community with the necessary data, educational research,
  42|                          professional development, support and resources needed to improve student performance.
  43|                          For additional information on any of these topics, please contact Karen Martin, Instructional Services Director, at 978-582-4122
  44|                          ext. 3112 or e-mail kmartin@lunenburgonline.com.
  45|                                                                                 SPECIAL SERVICES DEPARTMENT
  46|                                                                                       Elaine Blaisdell, Coordinator
  47|                                     There are two hundred and seventy-seven students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in
  48|                          Lunenburg. This represents 16.7% of our total student population. This percentage is less than the state average of 17%. Of the
  49|                          total number of students receiving services, fifteen students received services outside the district during the 2011/2012 school
  50|                          year. Of the fifteen students, thirteen are in day placements and two are in residential placements. Of the day placements, one
  51|                          is serviced in a Collaborative and the rest are in private day placements. Eighty-seven students identified with a disability
  52|                                                                                                   32

===PAGE 36=== (ocr, 57 lines)
   1|                     receive accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg.
   2|                                 This year as a district, students with Special Needs did reach the expected progress level to achieve adequate yearly
   3|                     progress. Students with disabilities at the elementary school level reached the mark for adequate yearly progress after not
   4|                      making it last year. This year we did not make AYP in certain subgroups at the Middle School level. This data can be found at
   5|                     the DESE website: (http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/ayp/ayp_report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=01620000&orgtypecode=5&#).
   6|                     Through our Pyramid for Success and by adding Power Blocks and Study Island to address the needs of students who are not
   7|                     making sufficient progress in the area of reading and math, the students showed nice improvement at the Elementary School
   8|                     level. The Pyramid for Success follows a Response to Intervention model which directly targets students identified through the
   9|                     district's progress monitoring systems.            Three tiers of intervention provide students with direct interventions with varying
  10|                     degrees of intensity to address the areas in which they are struggling. Teachers and Administrators in each building are
  11|                     continuing to work on the implementation of their five-year plans to provide a series of services and interventions across the
  12|                     disciplines and in the areas of social/emotional learning to meet the needs of all of our learners.
  13|                                The staff continues to use the triple threat: results teams, understanding by design for lesson planning and GEMS
  14|                     curriculum to accomplish our goals of continuous improvement. At team meetings parents and teachers engage in active
  15|                     discussions about the data and research-based practices necessary to address individual student's strengths and weaknesses.
  16|                     They continue to think outside the box in developing plans that meet the unique needs of each student in the least restrictive
  17|                                Instructional teaming across the district continues, and teams continue to refine their abilities to differentiate
  18|                    instruction and lessons that support students in achieving their educational goals through continued professional development
  19|                    activities provided by the district. The district strives to provide common planning and professional development to build
  20|                     effective instructional teams across the district.
  21|                                Lunenburg's progress toward the Indicators monitored by the state can be found at
  22|                    http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/spedmap.aspx?orgcode=01620000&. At this site residents will find information relative to
  23|                    drop-out rates, graduation rates, participation and performance of students with disabilities in state testing,
  24|                    suspension/expulsion data for students with lEP's, Early Childhood Transition Data, and Transition Data for students graduating
  25|                    with disabilities.

  27|                    teachers and administration. All parents and community members can join the group, which currently consists of parents of
  28|                    students receiving Title I services and parents of students with disabilities. Meetings are held the second Tuesday of each
  29|                    month, 6:30 PM, at the Lunenburg High School Library. The executive board is made up of Chairperson: Kelly Harvey and
  30|                    Treasurer: Pami Terren. Several events and presentations were organized by the PACSAL for both parents and teachers in
  31|                    Lunenburg this year. Topics covered include Physical Activity by Sandy Laserte, Executive Functioning by Gail Okerman,
  32|                    Growth Mind Set by Karen Martin, and Rights and Responsibility in Special Education by Elaine Blaisdell. The PACSAL had a
  33|                    viewing of Including Samue/ this year, and it was well attended by parents, staff and students. The PACSAL continues to provide
  34|                    information                   disabilities                              organization.               For         more        information,                  to:
  35|                    https://sites.google.com/allunenburgschools.net/special-services/pacsal.
  36|                                In conclusion, the Lunenburg Public Schools provides a variety of special needs services to meet the varying needs of
  37|                    our students in the least restrictive environment. We have an experienced, hard working staff that works together with parents
  38|                    to provide these varied services. Our parents are equal partners in our efforts to develop challenging and focused educational
  39|                    programs for children. We remain committed to the Lunenburg Public Schools mission, "We prepare our students for lifelong
  40|                    learning and responsible community membership." This is a total community effort and we encourage ALL Lunenburg residents
  41|                    to join with us to create positive experiences and futures for our children.

  45|                                                                             LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
  46|                               The Lunenburg Primary School is a child-centered school devoted to providing quality early childhood education for all
  47|                    students. We are a community of teachers, students, and parents working together to create a safe and respectful environment
  48|                   for children. Our current knowledge of child development guides the school's beliefs and practices and provides the foundation
  49|                   for student learning. This vision for our students keeps us focused on the goals and objectives of the school.
  50|                               We started the 2011-2012 school year with 345 students in 15 classrooms for an average class size of 23. Our two
  51|                    Preschool classrooms have a combined total of 37 students. We persistently strive to find better ways to meet the collective, as
  52|                    well as the individual needs of our students. We piloted a new handwriting program in our five full-day Kindergarten classrooms
  53|                    called Handwriting Without Tears. Early results are quite positive! Our Professional Development focused on differentiating our
  54|                    Math Curriculum and Response to Intervention strategies. We refined our "best practices" in order to meet the needs of ALL
  55|                   learners. We worked with experts in the field to provide our staff with opportunities to be "coached" in Mathematics, Literacy,
  56|                    and Inclusive Practices. Teachers were trained to use a new student information management system called PowerSchool. Staff
  57|                                                                                             33

===PAGE 37=== (ocr, 55 lines)
   1|                          is able to take attendance as well as complete report cards electronically. The new parent portal allows parents to see their child's report card, account balances, and attendance online.
   2|                                      The Primary School community continues to strive toward academic excellence. Common assessments are in place in
   3|                          the fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These common assessments
   4|                          serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and drive instruction. We are now able to track progress throughout
   5|                          the year in a faster, more efficient manner in order to improve instruction by using software to organize our data.
   6|                                      Our Pyramid of Success works to ensure that our students have the services they need to be successful. We continue
   7|                          to refine the Response to Reading and Math Intervention three-tiered process. The first tier is the largest and is fitted for
   8|                          universal instruction. Many best practices of teaching are used in this tier. The second tier is based on a targeted individual,
   9|                          and further, more intensive interventions to help that individual to be successful are used. The third tier is the smallest and
  10|                          involves special services with very intense interventions. Successful interventions are now in place in both Reading and Mathematics.
  11|                          Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive of each of our members. We have
  12|                          high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and recognize the importance of building a community where members feel a
  13|                          sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute. Our students have a right to be in an environment which is safe, promotes learning, and enables them to achieve success.
  14|                                     Successful fundraising, with the support of the Parent/Teacher organization, has allowed us to integrate a number of
  15|                          field trips into the curriculum. Students have attended live performances and visited museums and other cultural venues. In
  16|                          addition, we have experienced performances in the areas of Performing Arts, Native American culture and Animal Habitats. The
  17|                         Scholastic Books fundraising program has allowed us to purchase many books and other resource materials in order to support
  18|                         our Literacy program throughout the school. We also maintained partnerships with businesses that support education such as
  19|                         Hannaford Helps Schools, Target, Terra Cycle, Box Tops for Education (General Mills), and Campbell Soup. Our recycle efforts
  20|                         this school year included the elimination of plastic utensils in the cafeteria, as well as reducing the use of Styrofoam. We
  21|                         continue to recycle foil pouches and collected over 12,000 during the 2010-2011 school year.
  22|                                    Student Success Night was held for all Kindergarten, Grade 1 and 2 students, and their families in the spring of 2012.
  23|                         This night serves as the vehicle for students to display/articulate their strengths as a learner. To prepare for Student Success
  24|                         Night, students worked with their classroom teachers to identify and articulate their strengths in four areas: academics, habits
  25|                         of mind, creativity, and citizenship. Examples of student work in these areas were displayed in the classrooms. Students acted
  26|                         as tour guides for their families on Success Night.
  27|                                    The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school issues, review the annual budget for the school, and
  28|                         develop the School Improvement Plan, as well as make revisions to the school's handbook.

  26|                                                                           T.C. PASSIOS ELEMENTARY SCHOOL
  27|                                                                                         Sara Lane, Principal
  28|                      of approximately 24 students. The 2011-2012 Thomas C. Passios Elementary School year began with 358 students in 15 classrooms for an average class size
  29|                                 The school year began with great news. TCP made Adequate Yearly Progress (AYP) in student growth in the aggregate
  30|                     and Secondary Education (DESE). as well as in all of the subgroups and was designated a Commendation School by the Massachusetts Department of Elementary.
  31|                                 The wonderful achievement of the students was a testament to all of the hard work that had been undertaken
  32|                     throughout the school year by all constituencies in the school community to find better ways to meet the collective as well as
  33|                     individual needs of all of our students. Monumental student growth and improvement was attributed to many different initiatives
  34|                     that contributed to the success of the students including the establishment of a school mantra: "Good Enough Is NOT Good
  35|                      Enough; Strive for Excellence" - as well as the launch of the Trifecta, a three-prong program that was designed to support
  36|                     traditional classroom teaching by tailoring ancillary programs to meet the dynamic learning needs of all students.
  37|                                While all of the staff, parents, and students of TCP celebrated and were credited in making AYP, the celebration was
  38|                    tempered with the knowledge that regardless of the positive inroads achieved by the students, the school is not out of the woods yet. No Child Left Behind mandates that a school must continue to demonstrate growth for two years in a row to rid itself
  39|                     of the needs improvement and corrective action status. Furthermore, the deliberate commitment to continuous improvement
  40|                     cannot diminish because despite making significant strides in growth and gains in achievement, the school's performance
  41|                     the course. targets were not met. Rather, the TCP MCAS scores demonstrate that the work has just begun and that everyone needs to stay
  42|                                Improved MCAS scores at TCP demonstrate that education is a journey and like any worthwhile endeavor there are
  43|                     always obstacles in the road to success that must be surmounted. Change is difficult and takes time and commitment as well as
  44|                     effort. For something to work, every member of the school community must be dedicated to working together to make it work
  45|                    100 percent of the time. Every decision, every action, every result must be aligned with a shared vision that is consistent and
  46|                    driven by the pledge to do the right things for students. High expectations must be made clear and individuals must buy into the
  47|                    understanding that success is predicated upon everyone contributing. People must be given the knowledge, resources, and time
  48|                    to accomplish meaningful goals. All constituencies must be cognizant of the path as well as the destination.
  49|                                Accordingly, during the 2011-12 school year, programs like the Trifecta were tweaked and enhanced by a longer school
  50|                    day, a modified 45-minute block schedule that supported common planning time, a revitalized Power Block that was more data
  51|                    driven, and the development and deployment of a Research Skills class using the Independent Investigation Method (IMM) that
  52|                    supports the growth mindset as well as the tenants of the School-wide Enrichment Model (SEM). The staff is also using Teach
  53|                    Like a Champion by Doug Lemov as an anchor book to examine best practices and share ideas at staff and common planning
  54|                                                                                              35

===PAGE 39=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                           time meetings. The specials block was expanded to accommodate a technology class and homeroom teachers were assigned
   2|                           weekly time in the computer lab to give students an opportunity to conduct Internet research, use Study Island, and explore the
   3|                           classroom teaching by tailoring programs to individual needs and offering different modalities to access knowledge and support
   4|                           academic success. The goal of the initiatives is to provide greater opportunities to engage students in the learning process
   5|                          through the use of technology, intensive directed instruction, and project-based learning. While one of the original objectives of the Trifecta initiative was to increase MCAS test scores at all levels, the larger aim was to proactively engage more students in
   6|                           the learning process by appealing to different learning styles. The TCP Trifecta was specifically designed to provide students
   7|                           with multiple ways to support learning both in and out of the classroom. It was based on the premise that the more choices
   8|                           students are provided with at home and at school, the more engaging, successful, lasting, and meaningful the learning
   9|                           experience. The various programs that have been implemented at TCP were intended to offer greater rigor, challenge, and enrichment that support the academic and social-emotional success of all students.
  10|                                      Over the 2011 summer, staff participated in enhanced Response to Intervention (RT training) and throughout the
  11|                          school year staff members had opportunities to work together to build inclusive practices. RTI initiatives and consultations at
  12|                          TCP have been expanded and resulted in the establishment of a Teacher Support Team (TST) that meets twice a week to assist
  13|                          teachers in dealing with the learning and behavioral issues of their students. Additionally, teachers are building an RT/ Survival
  14|                          Kit that contains examples of strategies and tactics that may be used to address dynamic learning needs.
  15|                                     From July 5-August 11, 2011, a Project-Based Learning Summer Adventures program was implemented featuring
  16|                          three two-week sessions of classes that ran four days a week for two hours each day from 9-11AM. The summer classes cost
  17|                          $75.00 and offerings during the different sessions included Quidditch, Environmental Explorers, Techno Team, Art, Drama, Website Design, Babysitting and Childcare, Cake Decorating, Drama, and Mad Science. All of the TCP PBL classes were
  18|                          interdisciplinary, heterogeneous, and opened to all students entering or leaving grades 3, 4, and 5. Students who could not
  19|                          afford the fees were given scholarships. The activities were purposefully designed to be linked to life and encouraged authentic real-world connections.
  20|                          In the 2011-2012 school year, the CP after school Project-Based Learning classes continued. During the first session, classes
  21|                         were offered in Writing Comic Books, Babysitting and First Aid, Cooking, Cupcake Decorating, and Mad Science. During the
  22|                          second session, classes were offered in Homework Club, Power Up - Power Down, Music Movement, Clue Games, Mad Science
  23|                          and Arts and Crafts. To offset the costs of materials and personnel, students paid a $20.00 program fee, which was waived if needed, and were provided with additional day care after the one-hour classes, until 5:30 PM at no additional cost.
  24|                         The PBL program is grounded in the use of in-depth, high-interest, and rigorous projects to facilitate learning, attain practical
  25|                          knowledge, and provide academic enrichment. PBL focuses on student-centered inquiry to respond to a complex issue,
  26|                          problem, or challenge that links learning to real life. The PBL initiative allows students to work in groups or individually and
  27|                          encourages them to come up with creative ideas or realistic solutions and make presentations on their findings. All group
  28|                         learning is self-directed with the teacher acting as a facilitator. All of the PBL classes are infused with opportunities for research,
  29|                         reading, writing, and creative problem solving. The PBL programs have a culminating activity which is held during the last week. Parents were invited to attend demonstrations.
  30|                         In addition to PBL classes, the use of Study Island, an instructional and diagnostic web-based resource, was continued. The
  31|                         secure 24/7 Study Island website is accessed with individualized IDs and passwords and provides students with lessons in both
  32|                         the math and ELA content areas that assist in the procurement and mastery of content. A state-of-the-art, multimodal resource, Study Island supported the after-school and/or at home use of technology to enhance the acquisition of skills as well as develop
  33|                         proficiencies assessed on standardized tests. Teachers also used the Study Island program to build formative assessments that
  34|                        were utilized to determine the pre- and post-mastery of skills. Additionally, a Study Island facilitator was responsible for
  35|                         generating user reports, monitoring access, working with teachers to infuse lessons into content areas, and troubleshooting problems.
  36|                         The final component of the TCP Trifecta was the continuation of the Power Blocks that homogeneously grouped students based
  37|                         upon their needs, an examination of data from formative and summative assessments, and teacher input and recommendations.
  38|                         During Power Blocks, students are divided into small groups based upon their needs and abilities and each group was provided
  39|                        with strategies to help improve their achievement, increase their stamina, and acquire the test-taking techniques that will make
  40|                        them more successful. Student progress was continually assessed and monitored through the use of standardized tests, practice tests, and teacher feedback.
  41|                         By design, the Power Block class time was divided into reading, writing, and math. All students are given warm-up activities,
  42|                         short stand-alone lessons with lots of practice, modeling, and quick assessments to insure that learning occurred through the
  43|                        creating, retaining, and transferring of knowledge. Power Block utilizes teachers, tutors, and aides to provide directed
  44|                        instruction in content areas while emphasizing the building and maintaining of skills through the use of best practices, enrichment, and linking learning to real life.
  45|                        Additionally, work continued to empower students to assume more responsibilities by expanding the Principal's Council,
  46|                        improving recycling, initiating the Read Every Day (R.E.D.) program, establishing learning contests, scheduling school spirit days, and creating staff appreciation events.
  47|                                                                                                  36

===PAGE 40=== (ocr, 52 lines)
   1|                       Professional Development continued to be a fundamental tenant of continuous school improvement. The staff was committed to
   2|                       making the Trifecta a successful program. They were members of the ELA Taskforce and teachers learned to use PowerSchool
   3|                       to generate report cards, cutting turn-around time between grades closing and report cards being issued. The use of
   4|                       PowerSchool ensured a consistent format for report cards and expedited the use of report card data. The use of PowerSchool
   5|                       reduced data redundancy and updating errors. It also facilitated queries and data retrieval. Moreover, through the PowerSchool
   6|                       parent portal, parents are able to access their child's grades, attendance, content standards, lunch balances, and soon, the
   7|                       school bulletin.
   8|                       Brain Gym, a movement-based learning program, was utilized daily and school-wide as a way to encourage students to
   9|                       experience the use of intentional movement to improve sensorimotor skills and achieve personal goals. The Brain Gym concept
  10|                       counters sedentary, passive behavior. It is based on the importance of play and physical exercise as a way to engage learners
  11|                       by carrying out certain movements, like touching an elbow to a knee. The use of Brain Gym activities, each of which takes about
  12|                       a minute to do, are believed to create pathways in the brain and help activate the brain for the optimal storage and retrieval of
  13|                      information.
  14|                       The TCP Spelling Bee was held on Wednesday, February 8, 2012, and the winner, Jill Le, moved on to the regional spelling bee.
  15|                       The annual Lip Sync Soiree was held on May 30.
  16|                       Read Across America Day was held on Dr. Seuss's birthday, March 2, 2011. TCP teachers asked community members as well as
  17|                       other teachers to come into their classrooms and share their favorite stories with the students.
  18|                       The goal of all the new and time-tested programs and activities is to continue to support all of our students in attaining the skills
  19|                       that they need to become successful, contributing citizens in the global economy. We wish to further enable our students to
  20|                      collaborate, take risks, expand knowledge bases, and incorporate new tactics, technologies, and strategies into the learning
  21|                      process. Our belief is that students' skills need to be continually monitored, affirmed, reinforced, linked to life, and practiced in
  22|                      meaningful ways that promote achievement and success in and out of school.

   1|                    meets after school three times a week for 40 minutes a day until the end of May. The program is available to any
   2|                    student at T.M.H.S. and has been well attended by students striving towards success.
   3|                                We make a positive impact on the students we serve through offering a variety of different programs
   4|                    developed to promote social and emotional growth, self-awareness and community service. We continue to provide the
   5|                    Developmental Design program to grades six and seven to deliver our social/emotional curriculum. This program works
   6|                    by students meeting with teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem-solving skills, as well as
   7|                    to develop empathy and promote positive self-esteem. The results from this program have been a dramatic reduction in
   8|                    stress and anxiety-related nursing referrals. We continue to refine and enhance the program to incorporate anti-
   9|                    bullying lessons and meet the intricate social needs of our middle school students.
  10|                                All of these programs have been developed to allow students to showcase their talents and abilities, grow and
  11|                    improve as individuals and promote community outreach for the younger generation.
  12|                                Turkey Hill Middle School for the second straight year is a Great Books Foundation Model School, based on
  13|                    our excellence in instruction and ability to provide meaningful opportunities for the development of critical thinking
  14|                    skills. For the second straight year our faculty has been collaborating with the Great Books program experts on how to
  15|                    refine our instruction and improve writing skills throughout the curriculum. The feedback provided by the model school
  16|                    experts is positive and T.H.M.S. continues to excel in the targeted areas of writing and critical thinking when compared
  17|                    to other model schools in the country.
  18|                                Turkey Hill Middle School continues to provide educational services and support that ensure student success
  19|                    and promote lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to our success are our
  20|                    talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our
  21|                    teaching and learning and, most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning. Together we
  22|                    continue to make a difference in the lives of students.

  14|                                                                                          HIGH SCHOOL
  15|                                                                            Joseph P. Dillon, Interim Principal
  16|                      In July 2011, LHS Principal Mike Barney took a similar position at Mass Academy in Worcester and was replaced by
  17|                      Interim Principal Joseph Dillon for the 2011-2012 school year. During the first week of school, orientation meetings
  18|                      were held for all grades with an emphasis on the state's new bullying legislation and what the expectations were for all
  19|                       LHS students. In early September, a gth grade orientation night was held with guest speaker Julie Lesure from the
  20|                       Worcester County District Attorney's office who discussed issues such as responsible Internet use, Facebook, cyber-
  21|                       bullying and Internet safety. The evening was capped off with a question and answer forum led by four LHS students.
  22|                      NEASC ACCREDITATION
  23|                       On December 1, 2011, LHS submitted a Special Progress Report to the New England Association of Schools and
  24|                       Colleges (NEASC) with an emphasis on the standards of Curriculum and Community Resources for Learning. In the
  25|                       area of curriculum, all LHS content areas identified the primary and secondary academic, social, and civic expectations
  26|                      for which they would be responsible to ensure that each student at LHS would have regular opportunities to practice
  27|                       and achieve these expectations. Additionally, a curriculum template was developed for all curricula which included the
  28|                      following; units of study with enduring understandings, essential questions, concepts and skill, the school's learning
  29|                       expectations, and instructional strategies that include the use of school-wide analytic and course specific rubrics.
  30|                       The report also included technology upgrades that took place in 2011-2012. These upgrades included:
  31|                                  •  Fifteen multimedia carts were purchased for HS. Each cart is equipped with a laptop, projector,
  32|                                        document camera and speakers. An additional fifteen carts are requested as part of the FY 13 Capital
  33|                                        Plan.
  34|                                  • Created two virtual servers, eliminating the need for multiple servers.
  35|                                  • Completed work to provide wireless LAN throughout the school.
  36|                                 • Updated all computers to MS Office 2010.
  37|                                 • Rewired many of the high school rooms.
  38|                                 • Replaced computers in the following labs: Rooms 103, 105, 204 and the library.
  39|                                  • Purchased IPads for administrative staff.
  40|                                  • The district contracted with PowerSchool as the new student management system for the Lunenburg
  41|                                       School District.
  42|                            Regarding the Community Resources for Learning standard, the report focused on the shift from a regionalization
  43|                            discussion to a reconfiguration study.                  At the July 11, 2011, Lunenburg School Committee meeting, a
  44|                            recommendation was made by the School Committee Chair to form an advisory committee to explore
  45|                            reconfiguration as an option to address facility concerns at Lunenburg High School.
  46|                            A six-member advisory committee for district reconfiguration was formed in September of 2011. The charge of the
  47|                            committee was to identify the long-term educational opportunities and challenges around the reconfiguration, to
  48|                            identify organizational and facility challenges, and to articulate the educational opportunities and communicate to
  49|                            the public. In the October 19, 2011, draft of the advisory committee's findings and recommendations, the
  50|                            committee supported reconfiguration to a new 6-12 facility. In February of 2012, the Lunenburg School Committee
  51|                                                                                                 40

===PAGE 44=== (ocr, 54 lines)
   1|                          voted unanimously to accept the advisory committee's recommendation to support reconfiguration to a new or
   2|                          renovated 6-12 facility.
   3|                                                                                    MCAS RESULTS
   4|                     In 2011, LHS demonstrated solid gains in all MCAS content area results. Kudos to both students and teachers for their
   5|                     effort and commitment to continued progress. Using combined percentage bands of the advanced and proficient
   6|                    range, LHS scored 96% in ELA (compared to 84% state). In Mathematics, LHS scored 94% advanced/proficient
   7|                    (compared to 77% state. In Science/Technology/Engineering, LHS scored 91% advanced/proficient (compared to 67%
   8|                     state). We are extremely proud of our students and teachers for their continued improvement, and we will work to
   9|                     move all students to the level of proficiency as measured by MAS andour goal of college and career ready as they
  10|                     move through our curriculum.
  11|                     We were also very pleased to learn that 35 members of the Class of 2012 at Lunenburg High School received the state's
  12|                     John and Abigail Adams Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance
  13|                     puts them at the top 25 percent of their district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and
  14|                     Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English and Math exams. We also had one student who has advanced
  15|                     as a finalist in the National Merit Scholarship competition, and three students who received a letter of commendation.
  16|                     Thirty-one students also earned AP scholar awards for their exceptional achievement on Advanced Placement exams.
  17|                                                     HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
  18|                     Homecoming was once again a great town-wide celebration, culminating with a beautiful parade complete with floats
  19|                    representing all four high school classes. Many of our sports teams qualified for tournament and district play, and
  20|                     several school records in track were shattered by our talented student-athletes. LHS will be represented in the All-State
  21|                     Track by several of our outstanding female competitors. Mock trial, Latin club, marching band, jazz band and the math
  22|                     team represented LHS in numerous competitions. LHS was proud to announce that eight of our students were selected
  23|                     for the music districts, which is the highest number in recent history. Two of our students, one in band and one in
  24|                     chorus, were selected for the All-State competition. The 45th annual Competitive Class Plays and Freshmen Speeches
  25|                     delighted the audience, and the original play Miss Wallace Rhymes with William put on by the Class of 2012 was the
  26|                     highlight of the evening.
  27|                                                                                COMMUNITY BUILDING
  28|                     National Honor Society successfully supported the annual "Toys for Tots" drive during the holidays, helping to provide
  29|                     joy to needy children in conjunction with the local Marine Corps. They also organized the annual Red Cross blood
  30|                     drive in March. The Renaissance committee hosted the annual academic rally in November honoring LHS students
  31|                     who have excelled in the classroom. Our Global Issues and Community Arts classes have been busy with community
  32|                     service learning projects, raising awareness, raising money and collecting items. A highlight of the community service
  33|                     club involved the planning, design and implementation of a community ice rink. Students from Mr. Truax and Mr.
  34|                     Koziol's math class presented their plan at a school committee meeting in January of 2012. Also, the LHS hockey team
  35|                     held a fundraising campaign in support of Billy Woitowicz, a former Groton-Dunstable Hockey player who was killed
  36|                     while serving in Afghanistan.
  37|                     PROFESSIONAL DEVELOPMENT
  38|                     The staff at Lunenburg High School focused their professional development activities in 2011-2012 working on several
  39|                     continuing as well as new initiatives. All teachers spent professional development time reviewing and revising their
  40|                     course syllabi to conform to the new curriculum template adopted in the summer of 2011. In the Fall of 2011, the staff
  41|                     was introduced to a Looking at Student Work (LASW) initiative that allows teachers from all content areas to
  42|                     collaborate on student work for the purpose of ensuring consistency in assessment practices. In addition, teachers in
  43|                     the English and History Department spent time during the 2011-12 school year developing interdisciplinary units.
  44|                     These units will be piloted during the Spring and Fall of 2012.

   1|                                       MONTACHUSETT REGIONAL VOCATIONAL TECHNICAL SCHOOL (MONTY TECH)
   2|                                                                     Steven C. Sharek, Superintendent-Director
   3|                     I am honored to serve as Superintendent-Director of Monty Tech, a school that has transformed secondary education in
   4|                      North Central Massachusetts, bringing to life lessons learned in a classroom, at the workplace, and in the community.
   5|                      This school exemplifies what I believe education is all about - a commitment to excellence and the provision of a
   6|                      relevant learning experience.
   7|                      A Monty Tech education is grounded on workforce trends and labor markets, balanced by a rigorous academic
   8|                      curriculum. Our students are prepared not only to enter the workforce, but to also continue their education by enrolling
   9|                      in college. In fact, while 44% of the Class of 2011 is currently employed in a field related to their Monty Tech trade, 43%
  10|                      of 2011 graduates are currently seeking higher education.
  11|                      Compiling information for the 2011 Annual Report has been an exercise of great value for me. As a new administrator,
  12|                      it has provided me with an opportunity to reflect on the achievements and activities of our students, faculty and staff. It
  13|                      has afforded me the opportunity to visit with thoughtful partners in vocational education, and has given me great
  14|                      insight into the achievements of the 2010-2011 academic year. Some of the more notable highlights include:
  15|                      • 100% of the Class of 2011 successfully passed the state-required MCAS exam in English, Math and Biology.
  16|                     • The Class of 2011 earned a 93% placement rate: 62% college acceptance or advanced technical training, 13%
  17|                          career bound in field, 9% career bound out of field, and 7% military.
  18|                     • Seventy-eight members (25%) of the Class of 2011 were awarded the John and Abigail Adams Scholarship for
  19|                           outstanding performance on the MCAS examination.
  20|                     • 100% of Dental Assisting graduates passed the Dental Assisting National Board exams in both Radiation Health
  21|                           and Safety and Infection Control.
  22|                     • 2011 graduate Alicia McKean was the first student in the school's history to be named a National Achievement
  23|                           Scholar, and was the first to receive the coveted Presidential Scholar Award, providing a "full-ride" to attend
  24|                           Boston College.
  25|                     • Eleven students advanced through local, district and state competitions to earn the right to compete at the
  26|                           National SkillsUSA Conference in Kansas City, Missouri. The Monty Tech delegate was awarded four silver medals
  27|                           and one bronze medal.
  28|                      • Victoria Holbert was elected to the office of National SkillsUSA President.
  29|                           summer experiences, as well as monthly stipends.
  30|                      • Monty Tech was selected as one of only sixteen schools in the country and the only vocational school in the
  31|                           nation to have a student science experiment launch into space aboard the last shuttle flight of the U.S. Space
  32|                           Program, STS-135.
  33|                      Much like districts across the state, Monty Tech has been affected by the nationwide economic recession. Maintaining
  34|                      high standards of fiscal responsibility, with an eye on our over-arching mission of increasing student achievement and
  35|                      understanding, this school has been able to maintain our high standards of academic success, while presenting a
  36|                     budget that is fiscally conservative. Our FY11 Annual Budget was unanimously accepted by each of the eighteen
  37|                     member communities, and reflected less than a 1% increase over the 2009-2010 Educational Plan. We recognize that
  38|                     we are in the midst of some of the most difficult economic times faced by public education and, as a result, we will
  39|                     continue to strive to seek creative ways to reduce member town assessments.
  40|                     Thank you for your interest in our 2011 Annual Report, your continued support of vocational education in North Central
  41|                     Massachusetts, and for providing our students - your students - with the resources that enable them to become
  42|                     successful, contributing members of our communities.
  43|                                                                                       Our Mission
  44|                     Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocationall Technical School with the skills, knowledge, and
  45|                     abilities to bel a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.

   6|                                                                                           Leadership
   7|                         There were major changes in the school's leadership during the 2010-2011 school year.
   8|                         James R. Culkeen, who served as Superintendent-Director at Monty Tech for eight years, resigned in December of 2010.
   9|                         Francine K. Duncan, the school's Director of Technology, was named Acting Superintendent-Director, a position she
  10|                        held for just over two months.                Dr. Frank R. Llamas,        retired Superintendent-Director of Smith Vocational and
  11|                        Agricultural High School in Northampton, became interim Superintendent-Director on February 14. I was appointed
  12|                        Superintendent-Director during the summer and began work on July 18.
  13|                        Katy Whitaker was promoted to the position of Development Coordinator.
  14|                        Monty Tech's October 1, 2010, enrollment included 1,400 students in grades nine through twelve. Students are
  15|                        represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (46), Ashby (42), Athol (122), Barre (37), Fitchburg
  16|                        (393), Gardner (193), Harvard (3), Holden (46), Hubbardston (53), Lunenburg (63), Petersham (6), Phillipston (20),
  17|                        Princeton (14), Royalston (21), Sterling (53), Templeton (104), Westminster (57), and Winchendon (115). The remaining
  18|                        twelve students were from out-of-district towns, including Clinton, Lancaster, Leominster, Orange and Townsend.
  19|                        The Guidance/Admissions Department received 736 applications for admission to the school. Of those, 636 were from
  20|                        students hoping to enter our incoming ninth-grade class. The balance of the applications came from students hoping
  21|                        to enter the school as upperclassmen. There were only a limited number of slots for students applying in either
  22|                        category. We admitted 382 freshmen. We admitted 28 students to the tenth- and eleventh-grade classes.
  23|                        Monty Tech continued to offer a variety of opportunities for students, parents and community members to learn about
  24|                        and visit the school. In October 2010, 1,110 district eighth graders participated in Tour Days. Students toured many of
  25|                        our twenty vocational/technical areas and learned about the challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and
  26|                        extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return at night, with
  27|                        their family members, to explore the facilities and talk with staff members. Career Awareness Night, as well as last
  28|                        spring's Open House, was attended by an estimated one thousand individuals per event, comprised of students,
  29|                        parents, and community members from throughout the district.
  30|                        The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) continued to attract large numbers of seventh and eighth grade students
  31|                        during the 2010-2011 school year. Due to the high enrollment, a third five-week session was implemented and this is
  32|                        our second year doing all three sessions. Participants had opportunities to attend hands-on after-school sessions in a
  33|                        variety of vocational/technical areas. A satisfaction survey rated this program 4.75 out of 5. Enrollment was 725,
  34|                        among the highest ever.
  35|                                                                                    Class of 2011 Awards
  36|                        Members of the Class of 2011 were awarded at least $52,225 in scholarships. The Monty Tech Foundation provided
  37|                       $21,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $100 to $1,500. The Foundation also awarded
  38|                        $6,000 to the practical nursing graduates. Once again, local and state organizations, as well as generous individuals,
  39|                       continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of donations. The School
  40|                        Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves, are grateful for this support.
  41|                        Articulation Agreements with local colleges continue to play an important role in helping our graduates enter college
  42|                        through credits earned at Monty Tech. Approximately 65% of our graduates entered a two-or four-year college. This
  43|                        number has continued to grow over the years.
  44|                                                                                       Financial Report
  45|                        Numerous challenges were faced during the development of the fiscal year 2010-2011 budget including rising health
  46|                       insurance, student transportation, and utility costs. Considerable effort was put forth by the School Committee,
  47|                       administration and staff to develop a cost-effective budget. The final fiscal year 2010-2011 Educational Plan totaled
  48|                       $22,225,324, which represents less than a 1% increase over the 2009-2010 Educational Plan.
  49|                        The District was audited in August 2011 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson, Heath
  50|                        and Co. from Greenfield, MA and an "excellent" report is anticipated.
  51|                                                                                    Grants and Contracts
  52|                                                                                                 44

   1|                      Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds assist in providing many educational
   2|                      and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2011, state and federal grant sources provided the school
   3|                      with $1,236,639. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services, Social Intervention and
   4|                      Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title I Support, Drug Free
   5|                      Schools, Perkins Occupational Education, and Summer Academic Support.
   6|                      In addition to state and federal grant sources, Monty Tech was also the recipient of private and competitive grant
   7|                      awards totaling $22,084. For Fiscal Year 2011, Monty Tech received $13,334 in grant funds from the Community
   8|                      Foundation of North Central Massachusetts, to support the Student Spaceflight Experiment Program.
   9|                      Massachusetts Space Grant Consortium and the North Central Massachusetts Workforce Investment Board also
  10|                      contributed to this ground-breaking program, by adding an additional $6,873 in grant funds. Finally, the school
  11|                      received I-Car grant funds (to be used toward instructional equipment) and a Lowe's Toolbox for Education Grant,
  12|                     totaling just under $2,000.
